1571. THOMAS JONES , JOHN HARVEY , and JOHN MARK—MAN , were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 3 s. the goods of Mark Whincup, from his person.
EDWARD LANOLKY ( police-constable L 148.) 1 was on duty in Greenwich park, on the 8th of June, in plain clothes, in company with another officer—I saw Jones take this handkerchief—I had observed the three prisoners together for half-an-hour, walking and talking together round a ring—I saw Jones take the handkerchief from Mr. Whincup's pocket, he walked away with Harvey—I took them, with the assistance of my fellow-officer—I found the handkerchief in Harvey's breeches—Markman stood still.
JOHN JONES ( police-constable S 77.) I was on duty in the fair, in plain clothes—I observed the prisoners for about half-an-hour walking through the fair—they went into the park—I observed the prisoner Mark. man attempt several women's pockets, and men's pockets occasionally—they then went round a ring, and had an opportuity of feeling or sounding person's pockets I then saw them, behind a gentleman, but did not see them take any thing—I took Jones and Mark man, and my brotherofficer took Harvey.
Markman. I was not in the park above twenty minutes, when that off cer ran up and took them, and then he took me—though I had not pasted five words with them. Witness. As we were taking them to the watch-house, I saw Markman trying to get somethiug out of his pocket—I searched him, and found nine shillings and four-pence on him.
Markman's Defence. I went to the fair with my father, and lost him, which was the reason of my being in the park—I had money enough to keep me from doing any thing of this sort.
JONES— GUILTY .—Aged 17.
HARVEY— GUILTY .—Aged 16. Confined Three Months.
MARKMAN— GUILTY .—Aged 15.

[trialAccount t18350615-1573]
1573. PHILIP JOHN SCOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of June, 1 pair of shoes, value 2 s. 6 d., and 1 pair of half-boots, value 3 s. 6 d., the goods of Edward Wall is, his master.
EDWARD WALLIS . I am a shoe-maker, and live in Lower Marsh, Lambeth. The prisoner was in my service for some weeks, and attended the shop—I missed a great quantity of shoes—on the 12th of June, the prisoner mentioned to my wife that he thought of leaving—she mentioned it to me—I said to him, "I understand you wish to leave, but having missed a great quantity of goods lately, I insist on seeing what you carry with you"—I told him to get his box down—I followed him up stairs—he brought the box down to my parlour, and unlocked it, and the first thing he pulled out was a silk waistcoat, then a pair of white trowsers, and in the pocket of them was a pair of new boots of mine, and also a quantity of leather, which I believe to be mine; but the boots I knew to be mine—he said somebody had put them into his pocket—his box was locked—he had the key—another box was unlocked, and there I found a new pair of shoes, and part of a piece of
galloon—he then said he did put them there himself—he said, on his way to the station-house, that he hoped I would forgive him, and that he did steal them both.
Cross-examined by MR. MAHON. Q. Did you tell him you were going to prosecute him? A. I did not—I did not tell him I would not prosecute him if he made a statement—I used no inducement to get a confession—I had been up stairs in his room an hour before I found the goods in his box—on my wife's telling me he intended to leave, I went to look under his bed, thinking he might conceal something there, but found nothing—I did not particularly look at the boxes—I saw them—I should know them again—I cannot say whether they had common locks or not—when they were brought down, he said, "Somebody has been at my trunks"—I had been up stairs a quarter of an hour before—Mrs. Goodings lodges at my house—she said that I had been to his box—she said she saw me at the boy's box—I certainly acknowledge that I went into hit room, to search his bed, and I saw one of the boxes—there was a bundle of dirty clothes there—I have known the prisoner many years—I do not think he was in my service in 1831—I will not swear he was not—I did not follow him to two or three shops, to bring him back to my employ—I know Mr. Decrow—he is the same trade as me—I never went there to him, nor did I speak to him in the shop—I have been in the shop, but never while he was there—I know Mr. Hale, in Pratt-street, Lambeth—the prisoner was there—I did not go to him there—I have been to Hale's shop, he was there at the time I went—I went on business—I did not speak to him about coming back to my service—he has called on me once or twice when I have, been standing at my door—I was never at Mr. Poulter's shop in my life—I do not know him—I know he was in Mr. Poulter's service—he came to me, and wished to return to me—I did not ask him—he wanted too high wages for me to take him again—I do not believe he ever lived with me but once before the last time—I will swear it.
Q. Were you at Epsom-races, this year? A. I drove my cart there—I had no words with the prisoner on that day—I did not a busehim, or swear at him—I had no quarrel with him whatever—the officer has the boots—they are children's boots and shoes—there is no mark besides "No. 7" on them, and I can swear to the manufacture, by the workmanship—I know the workmanship, and have the manufacturer here—there is no stamp on them or number, but I can swear to the make of them.
COURT. Q, What workmen do you keep? A. Several; I have a Wholesale manufacturer, who supplies me—they are not made in my house—he supplies other people as well as me—these are the boots—they have no private shop-mark of my own—I bought the shoes of another person, who makes goods, and travels from shop to shop to sell them—I missed a great number of boots and shoes of all sizes—when the prisoner produced the boxes they were locked—I had never seen them before he Drought them down—I never saw the inside of his box.
THOMAS AYLESBURY ( police-constable E 136.) I received the prisoner into custody, and produce the boots and shoes—he said he had taken the hoes, but not the boots—he said he did not know who put the boots in his pocket—he said he did not put the boots into his box or pockets—he said he had taken the shoes—this was at the police-station, after the prosecutor brought him there.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he tell you somebody had been at his box in
his room? A. He did not—I did not hear him say it—he said nothing to me about Mrs. Gooding.
EDWARD WALLIS re-examined. He acknowledged having put the galloon and shoes into the box, on the way to the station-house, before Ayletbury had him in custody—he acknowledged it at the station-house also.
JOHN TOBY . I made these boots, and make for a great number of people—I have sold the same sort and size as these to Mr. Wallis; but I cannot swear to having sold him these in particular—I went into Wallis's shop at the time the large box was brought down—the prosecutor was rather confused, and I unlocked the small box—the prisoner having given me the key; he owned to putting the shoes in, but not the boots—on the road to the station-house, he admitted putting the boots into his trowsers—Willis asked him how long ago he had stolen them—he said he could not tell—I asked him whether it was one week or three—he said, he supposed it was two or three.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not sold dozens of that sort to other people? A. Yes; but not in the neighbourhood—the prosecutor and I are friendly—he has paid me a good deal of money.
Prisoner's Defence, I have a witness to prove he put them into my box.
MARY ANN GOODING . I am married. I lodge in Wallis's house, in the next room to where the prisoner slept—I was in the house the day his boxes were brought down stairs—I saw Mr. Wallis in the boy's room that day—he did not remain there more than a minute or two—the door was open—it is not two yards from my room—I saw him settling something is the box—his hands were in the box, and he seemed settling something—it was only a momentary glance—the lid of the box was open—I did not see the boxes down stairs—it seemed a small dirty white box—I am positive it was open—I saw Wallis with his hands in it, at the side of the bedstead—the room is large enough to hold a bedstead, and no more—the box was on the side of the bedstead—in a few minutes afterwards, I saw the boy and Mr. Wallis running up stairs—the boy said, somebody had been at his box—I think Wallis was in the room when he said that, because I heard his voice—I went out on the stairs, and said to the boy, "Why, Mr. Wallis has;" thinking that the boy might think it was an impertinence of ours, our room being so near—I said this in Wallis's presence, for fear the prisoner should think that any of my family had taken the liberty of going into his room—there had been no quarrel between Mr. Wallis and me, till that day—I worked for him, and my husband worked for him—we have been very good friends till that day—and that day Wallis came up and asked me what business I had to tell the boy he had been to his box, and he denied it; but I stuck to it ever since that he was at the box.
COURT. Q. As you say there was only room for the bed in the room was the box against the bed? A. No; on the bed—I am not mistaken about Wallis being at the box—I saw the box open—I could not see the contents of it—I was standing on the landing, opposite the door—I cannot say I saw the lid; but I saw the box open—I did not see the inside of the lid—I saw part of the lid in a position to satisfy me it was open—I am not mistaken—I saw the box open, and his hand in it.
EDWARD WALLIS re-examined. I was never at his box in an open state in his room—the box stood under the bed—I did not remove it—I did not put my hands into it—the box was not on the bed while I was in the room—it has a flat lid—when the prisoner brought it down into the parlour
I saw it, it was a broken box—it was locked—the small one, containing the shoes, was locked; and the other one, containing the boots, was locked also—one he unlocked himself, and desired Toby to unlock the other of the boxes might be got at by force, without a key—it was a rickety deal box—this morning I had the officer to see the room—Mrs. Gooding swears she saw me open the box—it is impossible if I did do it, for her to have seen it—I shut the door while I was in the room—I went to look at his dirty linen, and see what he had got in the room; but his box I never looked into—my wife informed me he wanted to leave while I was absent, but I happened to come home unexpectedly.
Cross-examined. Q. If you merely wanted to look for dirty linen, why shut the door? A. I had no particular reason—I did not mind who saw it—Mrs. Gooding's room is four or five steps higher than the prisoner's—she told me that day that she saw me at the box, and I told her it was false.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1574]
1574. WILLIAM CAWLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of May, 3 hat leathers, value 6 d.; and five half leathers, value 1 s.; the goods of Benjamin Rider and another, his masters.
BENJAMIN RIDER . I am in partnership with my father—we live in Southwark. The prisoner was about five weeks in our employ—on the 29th of May I missed some hat leathers—I went to the prisoner's house in consequence of suspicion, and saw a female who I believe was his wife, on the 30th of May, and purchased of her three hat leathers, out of about eight dozen, which she produced—the prisoner had never bought any of us—I then informed my father, and went with a policeman and searched his house—I found three whole hat leathers in his parlour; and fifteen halves up stairs—I knew them to be ours by our stamping—we sell them in that states; but he never bought any—I have the machine that they were cut by, which enables me to know my own work—I only know it to be the prisoner's house in consequence of what his brother said—I never saw him there myself.
ALEXANDER KERR . I am a policeman. I searched the house which Mr. Rider took me to—I do not know it to be the prisoner's house—it was No. 11, Pitts-place—I never saw him there, or had any conversation with him about the property found there.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1575]
1575. JAMES EDMONDS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BODKIN and STURGEON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SIMPSON . I am a chandler, and live in St. Alban's-street, Lambeth. The prisoner came to my shop about half-past nine o'clock at night, on the 6th of June, and asked for half an ounce of tobacco—I weighed the tobacco—he gave me a counterfeit shilling—I told him it was a bad one—he said he had taken it by selling things in the street—I told him I much doubted it, and gave him in charge—I marked the shilling in the presence of the officer, and gave it into his hands.
JOSEPH METHERILL ( policeman L 142.) On the night of the 6th of June I was on duty near Mr. Simpson's shop—he called me and gave the prisoner in charge—he said he had given him a bad shilling—I searched him, and found another bad shilling on him and some duplicates.
JOHN FIELD . I am an inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. These are both counterfeit, and impressions from the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I took them by selling things in the street.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
Second Jury, before Mr. Baron Bolland.

The particulars of this case are of too indelicate a nature for publication.

[trialAccount t18350615-1577]
1577. FREDERICK MILLS and RICHARD GRAHAM were indicted for feloniously assaulting Maurice Hurley, on the 23rd of May, at St. Giles, Camberwell, putting him in fear, and taking from his person and against his will, 3 shoes, value 6 s.; 1 stocking, value 4 d.; and I crown; the goods and monies of the said Maurice Hurley.
MAURICE HURLEY . I live in Nelson-street, Camberwell. On Saturday, the 23rd of May, about half-past one o'clock, I was going home from my pay table—I work at Wapping—I was going down Nelson-street—within six or seven doors of my own lodging, I saw two men standing between me and the footway, which is very narrow—I went out in the road to pass them—one of them jumped across and collared me—the prisoners are the two men I saw, I am pefectly sure—I am a little short-sighted—Mills stepped across to me, collared me, and pulled me into the footway where the other man was—he asked me, who I was—I said, "A friend"—I had a bundle under my arm, and a crown-piece in my right-hand pocket, and sixpence in copper—I had half-a-sovereign in my left-hand waistcoat pocket, sewed up by itself—Mills asked me whether I was English or Irish—I said, "Irish"—he said, "B—t the Irish, I don't like them"—he told me then to give him a shilling and he would let me go—I told him I had no shilling about me, no more I had; and as I would not give him the shilling—I was facing him, he turned my face away, came behind me, and gave me a blow with his fist on the pole of the head, and sent me reeling into the road—I was striving to get up, and when I got my senses I cried out "Murder," for I was out of my senses, when he knocked me at first—he knocked me out of my senses—when I cried "Murder," he ran and knocked me down again, gave me two or three pelts, and said if I spoke another word out of my lips, I should be murdered on the spot—I do not know what the other man did while I was down; but when I got up they were both standing together over me—I told them I would not speak a word at all if they would let me go with my life—I did not care what they did, so that they let me go with my life—they then let me go—I lost a bundle—I do not know whether they took it or not—I lost it when they knocked me down at first—they remained on the spot a little after I got up, and I ran away towards my lodgings—when I had run a little bit from them, I put my hand in my pocket, and my crown-piece and some of the coppers were gone—to the best of my knowledge I heard them feeling in my pocket, but I do not know whether they took any thing out, but I lost the crown-piece, threepence in copper, and the things that were in the bundle—the halfpence and crown were loose in my pocket—they might have tumbled out—I ran away directly I got up—I did not stop to look for my bundle.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know what a drunken row is? A. I was not drunk—I offered to settle this for half a guinea.
NOT GUILTY .
Third Jury, before Mr. Baron Bolland.
[trialAccount t18350615-1578]
1578. WILLIAM GLANNON and FRANCIS CHAPPELL were indicted for feloniously assaulting Benjamin George Gill, on the 9th of June, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, part of a watch-chain, value 10 s.; 3 seals, value 30 s.; and 1 watchkey, value 6 d.; his property.—2nd COUNT, for assaulting with intent to rob him.
BENJAMIN GEORGE GILL . On Tuesday, the 9th of June, about half-past ten o'clock, I was at the Crown and Anchor, in the Kent-road, in the long room—I remained there till nearly half-past one o'clock—I had been drinking—I had two pints of ale and some cyder—I drank with one or two others who I knew—I did not have above half a quartern of cyder—I might drink a glass or two of ale with others—when I got up to go away I was about half-and-half, not drunk or sober—I was aware of what I was doing, and what was done to me—on coming down stairs, I saw Glannon, whom I have known from a child—I went up to the bar, and told him I was very glad to see him out of trouble, and I partook of a glass of gin from his hand—the other prisoner drank—I do not know him—I knew him by sight before, but not to have much acquaintance with him—I had frequently seen him before—he was in company with Glannon—after having the gin, I went outside the door, to go away—I left the house, and was followed by Chappell, who pretended to be very drank indeed—I had seen him in the house, and should not think he was drunk then—he ran up against me, and shoved me about—he shoved me up into one corner—Glannon was there at the time, and I was placed on a bench by him, on my back—my legs were put up in the air by some of them—I believe there were others besides the prisoners there—Glannon was lying on my chest, keeping me down on the seat, and I had something put into-my month—I thought it was a piece of old hat, or cloth—I was trying to call out "Murder," but could not from the pressure against my breast, and my mouth was stuffed full of something, what I cannot tell—I cannot say who did it—it prevented my hallooing and making a noise—at that time Chappell was at my fob, in which I had a silver watch, and three gold seals, a gold key, and gold chain—I made great resistance—I found my chain in Chappell's hands—it broke away in the scuffle—I cannot say whether it broke away, at that time or not—he had removed it from my person—I had a scuffle with him for the chain, and cannot say whether he dropped part of the chain, or I did, but it fell—I was lying on my back, and the chain was found on the bench, between my legs—it was broken, and part of it gone—ray trowsers were torn away to get at my watch—I said at the time that I was robbed of my watch, but it was found in my fob—Whitford, the policeman, was there—I saw both the prisoners in the custody of the police, and was quite positive they were the men, and said so—Glannon said at the station-house, that he hoped I should not press the case against them—I have been an old police-officer of Bow-street and Union Hall, for fourteen years.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you not discharged from Union Hall, for extorting money from licensed victuallers? A. On my solemn oath, I was not—I gave my situation up—I was not discharged at
all—it was a good situation—several went from the office, and I gave my situation up to save the others—to take the blame all on my own back—I have no doubt I had some part of the property which was taken away—eleven of us went away together—I have above 50 l. a year, and property in the Bank, to live upon—I have not been an informer against any publicans—not in London—I went down into the country once, to oblige a Magistrate, about a baker having ingredients in his place—that is the only thing I have done in the country—I did not fight with any body on this night—nobody called me an informer—Glannon's father was an officer at Union Hall, and was with me for years—he took my place after I left it—I did not fight with a man before I was laid on the bench—I am positive that I was sober enough to know what took place—I thought my watch was gone when I got up, but I found it in my fob afterwards—Glannon had hold of my arm, and said, "You have not lost your watch?"—as he was going to the station, he said he hoped I should not press the case against him, or something to that effect.
Q. Were you not going home, and did not Glannon say you should not go home, until he had proved you had not lost your watch? A. Nothing of the kind passed.
JAMES SMITH . I am a ladies' shoe-maker, and live in Trafalgar-place, Lock's-fields. On Wednesday morning, I was at the door of the Crown and Anchor, about half-past one o'clock, sitting on a bench outside—Gill came and sat down on the bench opposite to me—he told me there had been an attempt to rob him of his watch—I saw his chain and seals hanging out in the usual way—he did not pull his watch out—I gave him some advice, and he shortly afterwards shifted himself from sitting opposite to me, and came and sat down by my side, and he either put his chain and seals inside his flap or pocket, so as to conceal them—Chappell came up and pretended to be very much intoxicated—his hat fell off several times, and each time his hat fell off, he got nearer to the end of the table where the prosecutor was, and then he fell into the prosecutor's lap—he went from one seat to the other, across the table, to fall into his lap—the prosecutor put him out, and then he fell into his lap again—Glannon was sitting on the seat, and when he came, the two prisoners began to hustle the prosecutor—he was hustled on to a bench, on his back, and his feet upwards—he was surrounded by half a dozen persons—I jumped over the table to get out of the way—his legs were up in the air—I did not see any thing that they did to him—a constable came (No. 156, I believe) and took them—the prosecutor got off the bench, and said, "I have been robbed of my watch"—Glannon, I think, said, "Search him before he goes away from the place, as he says he has lost his watch"—the prosecutor instantly gave part of the chain and seals to the policeman, out of his left hand.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Gill? A. I never saw him before that night—he told me that an attempt had been made to rob him of his watch, before Chappell came and sat by his side—I did not see him laid on the bench—I did not go into the crowd to see him—I was before the Magistrate last Friday week, which was two days after this happened—the policeman asked me to go—I was taking half a pint of porter this night—I am married, but it was fair time—I had been at the public-house about half an hour—I drank a share of two pots of porter—I had not been drinking before in any public-house—I had part of a pint of beer at the County Terrace—I did not go to Greenwich—I did not go to the station-house to state this, because I did not want to lose my time—I did
not see Glannon pressing against the prosecutor's breast—Chappell was certainly not drunk—he put his finger up three times to knock his hat off—I had never seen Chappell before this—he fell into the prosecutor's lap, from going on one side of the seat to the other—he certainly fell as a drunken man would.
COURT. Q. Was any body with you at the time? A. Yes; Thomas had been drinking with me, and saw all this.
Chappell. Q. Did you see me put my finger to my hat? A. Yes—I was net asked about it at Union Hall.
GEORGE THOMAS . I am a shoe-maker, and live in Fryar-street. I was at the public-house with Smith—the prosecutor came from the public-house, up to where I was sitting, and pulled out his watch-chain—I told him to put it in again, and he put it into his fob—I could not see whether he put it all in—he pulled it out as far as it would come, so as leave the watch in his pocket—after this he sat down on the right hand, at a little distance from Smith—at that time there were from fifty to sixty persons sitting down in two rows of settles—there were others sitting at the same bench with us—I did not know them—there was one man sitting between the prosecutor and Smith—Chappell came and sat on my right-hand side, then moved round to my left-hand side, and appeared very dronk—he fell with his arm on the table, and his hat came off—I think it was sham, from his manner—he got up once, and appeared to be looking after something, and then he got up quite firm—he did not get up on his feet, but merely lifted himself up—he went towards the prosecutor—I did not see him do anything—the prosecutor moved himself, and Went towards the corner—I saw Chappell go towards him—what he did I do not know—four or five were round him when they were robbing him—I do not know Glannon at all—I got out of my seat, went a short distance, saw two policemen, and told them a man was being robbed—I had turned my head, and Smith said, "Don't you see they are robbing him?"—I saw them rummaging about—the policemen came and took the prisoners.
Cross-examined. Q. If any body had forced Gill away from where he was sitting, up into the comer, you must have seen it? A. Yes; he went of his own accord—there were from fifty to sixty persons sitting in the different settles at the time—the policemen appeared to come out of a settle—I did not see Glannon at all—I advised Gill to tuck in his seals—when he pulled them out, he said, "See, they have been making an attempt"—he said they had done it in the public-house—I had been drinking but not much—I went to the station-house after the prisoners were taken.
EDWARD WHITFORD ( police-constable P 164.) I was near the public-house—Thomas called me—I saw the prosecutor lying on his back, on a bench—I took Chappell—the prisoners were on each side of the bench, quite close to the prosecutor—my brother officers took Glannon—the prosecutor said he had been robbed of his watch, and gave me these seals, and part of the chain—Glannon insisted on my searching the prosecutor—I refused to do so, and he instantly collared the prosecutor, and would not let him go till we got to the station-house—on searching him there, he had not lost his watch; he found it in his fob, and part of the chain to it, where it had been broken—I did not hear the prisoners speak to each other on the road—they were locked up—Chappell was not drunk by any means.
Cross-examined. Q. Did either of the prisoners attempt to run away? A. Chappell did; Glannon was not in my custody—he was standing by
the side of Gill when I was called—I never saw the chain or seals in his possession—Gill gave them to me.
B. G. GILL re-examined. These are my seals and chain—there is very nearly half the chain gone—the men were first examined on Wednesday morning—there was only me and the constables before the Magistrates then.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell the Magistrate that Glannon said he hoped you would not press the charge against him? A. Yes, I did—a constable who is not here took Glannon to the station-house—he was so deaf that they would not bind him over—I do not mean to say that the Magistrate said that was the reason—he might hear what Glannon said—I cannot tell.
Q. Have you asked for any money not to prosecute? A. Not since they were committed—one party came to me before, and I said I did not wish to press the charge, knowing his father, if they would make good my watch and trowsers.
Chappell. Q. You say you took the seals out of my hand? A. I say they were in your hand—whether you dropped them, or I took them out of your hand, I cannot say—I could not see them in your hand, as I was lying on my back; but they were in it, as I had hold of it when you first made the attempt.
Glannon's Defence. I was at the public-house with two females, and two young men, handing the glass to the females—Gill came behind me, and snatched the glass out of my hand—he shook hands with me, and had share of five half-pints of gin with me at the bar—he went out, and came in again, and said a man had called him an informer, would I come out—I came out, and he began fighting with a man—I pulled him away, and he immediately charged this young man and me with stealing his watch—I said, "Have you lost your watch?"—he said, "Yes"—the policeman refused to search him—I took him into the road, and said he should go with me to the station-house—the policeman said that I was not to do that, but I insisted on holding him, and took him with me to the station-house, and insisted on his being searched—he knew very well where to find me at any time, if I had robbed him—he has been for three days before the bill was found, trying to get money to make it up—my sister said she had money to fee counsel, and he said if she would give it to him instead, he would make it up.
EMMA GLANNON . I am the prisoner's sister. I saw Gill on the Wednesday my brother was examined—he said he was very sorry this had happened, and he cried, and said it was a shame to rob a man who got his living by thieving; and if he had 3 l., he would not appear against my brother—I said I had not got the money—he said he would get them remanded, and would make an assault of it—he used some very bad language at the time—I saw him again last Thursday, as I was coming out of the New Court—he asked where I had been all day—I said I had been about the place—he said he had been looking for me, and had sent over the water for me—he said, "If you will give me the money you are going to give the counsel, I will throw the bill out—I will say I was very drunk"—he asked me to give him something to drink—I said I would—I had no money, and returned into the Court, and went out and treated him with a pot of half-and-half—I had no one with me.
B. G. GILL, re-examined. I declare this is all false—I have seen her every day here, and know her well—I told her before I went in to the
Magistrate, if they made good the trowsers and watch, I would not press the case—I did not say I would make an assault of it; but that I would not go near the place for the sake of old Jem, his father—I might mention 3 l., but not since they were committed—I drank with this girl—my wife is as fond of her as she is of her own daughter—and I drank with Glannon when he was going to prison—I did not tell him I was going into the country, and he would hear no more of me.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350615-1579]
1579. THOMAS WELLS was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM WATTS . I keep the Sun and Dubbs, in Coal Harbour-lane, Camberwell. The prisoner was my pot-boy for about seven weeks—it was his duty to receive money on account of beer, and to account for it.
SARAH BROWN . I have beer of Mr. Watts. On the 18th of May, I paid the prisoner 4 s. 9 d. for beer; on the 25th I paid him 1 s. 9 d,; and on the 26th I paid him 8 d. for his master.
WILLIAM WATTS . He never accounted to me for these sums.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18350615-1580]
1580. GEORGE PURCELL was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM THOMAS ROPER . I am a butcher, and live in Long-lane, Bermondsey. The prisoner was in my service—when he took goods out, if he received money, it was his duty to bring it home, to me—on the 2nd of April I sent a leg of mutton to Mr. Castles by the prisoner—it came to 4 s. 4 d.—he never accounted to me for it—on the 4th of April I sent some veal, which came to 3 s. 11 d.; he has never accounted for that—on the 17th of April I sent part of a shoulder of mutton, which came to 1 s. 4 d.; I never received that.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What charge did you make at the police-office? A. Of receiving this money and not giving it to me—these different charges were gone into before the Magistrates—I cannot say when I discovered this—it might be a week or a fortnight before I discharged him—I told him I had heard that he had received the money for a joint of meat from Mr. Castles—he did not tell me he had received it, and had lost a half-sovereign—he said so before the Magistrates—he did not tell me he was waiting for the rest of his wages—he staid with me a fortnight, because I am not conversant with business—I did not owe him any wages—he said he had got into some quarrel with the police, and he had to pay 2 l,; and he asked me to go and pay it for him, which I did—there was no agreement that it should be deducted from his wages—I should have deducted it how I could—he received his wages at different times—sometimes he took 5 s., sometimes more—he was left to sell meat when I was out of the way, and to account for it to Mrs. Roper—he was not at liberty to deliver joints of meat to his friends—this is part of an account of mine; here is 4 s. for a joint of meat—I do not know whether he delivered it, or the boy; but when the boy came in, the prisoner would sometimes say, "Have you got the money?" and he would say, "No," and I would say, "I shall put it down to your account"—here is a leg of lamb, 5 s. 10 d.—I believe that went to Mr. Litchmore, of Charlton—the prisoner represented him as his brother-in-law—the boy said it had not been paid for; and I said to the prisoner, "I shall put it down to your
account"—I would not swear I did not keep the prisoner three weeks in my service after this discovery.
COURT. Q. Do you mean you kept him three weeks after you found out the loss of this 4 s. 4 d.? A. It might be a fortnight—I never asked him for the money.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you mean to swear you did not speak to him about the leg of mutton? A. I told him the day I was at Union Hall that I understood he left a leg of mutton, and he had received the money—he said he had a hole in his pocket, and it got out—he said he had lost the money—I did not say I should deduct it from his wages—I do not think I spoke to him again about it—I did not to my knowledge—I discovered about the veal some days after—I have laid that on the 4th of April—I cannot tell when I discovered about it, or how long I kept him in my service after it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1581]
1581. GEORGE PURCELL was again indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM THOMAS ROPER . The prisoner was in my service—on the 18th of April—he took a leg of mutton to Mr. Adams, which came to 4 s. 10 d., and I believe on the Saturday week or Sunday week following, Mr. Adams had some more meat—I sent it by the prisoner, and sent an account of that and the previous leg of mutton—the prisoner came back and said Mr. Adams had paid for the leg of mutton—I said, "No," I had not received it—he said, "Yes, you did, Mr. Adams saw you"—I said to Mrs. Roper, "Scratch off this, and I will see further into it"—I am quite sure the 4 s. 10 d. was not paid to me.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was this the first thing you discovered? A. No—Mr. Adams lived two doors from me—I desired this to be taken off the bill, because the prisoner told me he had received it, and paid it to me—I did not go to Mr. Adams for three or four days to have it explained—it was on Sunday morning when the prisoner told me—I do not know why I did not speak to Mr. Adams on the Monday or the Tuesday—I might have forgotten it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1582]
1582. MARY FITZCUMMINS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of George Fisher, her master.
GEORGE FISHER . I live in Upper Stamford-street. The prisoner was in my service at the letter end of March—after she was gone I missed this handkerchief and some other things—I can swear this handkerchief is mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What mark have you on it? A. My initials were on it, but they have been picked out—here is the mark where the letter "F" has been—the policeman brought us some duplicates in about two months—I described to him the handkerchief and other things—the prisoner called on us, I believe, the day after she left the service, and it appears to have been pledged in that day.
WILLIAM BIRD ( police-constable L 24.) I took the prisoner. Some duplicates were found on her—this one belongs to this handkerchief.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you the person who searched her? A. No; she was searched by a female in the cell, who is not here—I was at the cell door, and looked through to see what was going on—there were
fifteen duplicates found on her—I cannot swear that this identical duplicate was found on her.
JAMES NASH . I am a pawnbroker, and live in King-street, Borough. The prisoner pledged this handkerchief with me on the 25th of March—I have the counter-duplicate.
Cross-examined. Q. I see it is pawned in her own name? A. Yes; at No. 37, Stamford-street—she is called a lodger on the ticket.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the handkerchief of a fellow-lodger.
GUILTY . Aged 30 .—Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor, being deserted by her husband.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18350615-1583]
1583. JEREMIAH HURLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; 10 sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 8 shillings; the goods and monies of Robert Chambers, from his person.
MR. MAHON conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT CHAMBERS . I am a Trinity House pilot, and live at St. Mawes, in Cornwall. On the 27th of May, I came up with a ship from the Downs—I brought it to St. Katharine's Docks, and on the 284h I received 10 l. 10 s. in gold at the bank—I changed the half-sovereign, and put the ten sovereigns in my handkerchief which was round my neck—I had some silver in my trowsers pocket—I lost my passage home by the steamer that day, and crossed over to Tooley-street to inquire for the Portsmouth van—I saw the prisoner on the road—he heard me inquire for the van, and he said, "I can show you where the van goes from"—he took me to the White Hart Inn—I did not go in; I laid my head aganist my hand, and stood talking to the ostler, while the prisoner went in—he came out again, and said there was no van going from there—I then asked him if I could have a bed there—he went in and said there was no bed to be had there—he then said, "Come along with me"—he took me by the elbow, and said, "I will show you where you can get a bed"—he took me to a small public-house in Fishmonger-alley—I had a glass of gin and water, and I gave him a pot of beer—I cannot say whether the gin and water was brought from the bar; but it was put on the table—the prisoner sat on my right side—I partook of the beer, and drank some of the gin and water—I then found I was going as fast as I could go, like a man going to die—I was as stupid as if I had drank twenty glasses of grog—I fell into a stupor, and I laid my head down—the prisoner was by my tide, close to me—there were two or three persons came in, but they said nothing to me—I am certain I had my money safe when I went there; the sovereigns were in my handkerchief—I paid my reckoning there from my pocket—I continued in that sleep for three-quarters of an hour, but I fell on the floor—I was awoke by the landlord and the policeman—the landlord said, "This man had money, but he has no money now to pay for a bed"—I said, "What! no money?"—I clapped my hand to my neck, and missed my handkerchief and money—I am sure the prisoner is the man who was with me; he was dressed shabbily.
Prisoner. Q. Did 1 show you where there were any places to go to? Witness. Did not you take me to the White Hart?—I have got a witness here to prove you went with me—there were no other persons near me but you—I was sober.
JOHN NYLETT . I was servant at the White Hart Inn. I remember, on the evening of the 28th of May, the last witness came and asked me
where the Portsmouth van went from—the prisoner came in with him—he spoke to me directly after.
Prisoner. Q. Where did you see me? A. When I stood at the gateway, you came up the yard—it was about seven o'clock.
ELLEN MAHONEY . I live in Gold-court, Tooley-street. My husband is a a labourer—the prisoner came to lodge with me three weeks ago—I do not know the day—he was not at home the next day, but he came the morning afterwards, and said, how much did he owe me—I told him—he paid me, and said he would treat me with a glass of brandy—he said he had picked up ten or eleven sovereigns the night before—he said they were in a handkerchief—my girl asked him where the handkerchief was—he said he had thrown it away—he chucked me 3 l. 10 s., and said he was going to have a row with a young man—he had been rather shabby in his dress before that—he had been accustomed to sell needles and books for his living—I saw some papers with him, but I do not know what they were—he went and had a row with Sullivan—Sullivan then came with him into my place—the prisoner had a new jacket, waistcoat, hat, shirt, and trowsers—he dressed himself in them, and went out to buy a watch—I saw four sovereigns in his possession that morning—it was after he bought the clothes and the watch that he gave me the 3 l. 10 s.
Prisoner. Q. Did I tell you it was in a handkerchief that I found this money? A. Yes—there was nobody but my children in my house with me.
THOMAS SULLIVAN . I belong to the City of Glasgow steam-boat, and lodge with Mahoney. I saw the prisoner on the 29th of May, when I went down to wash my face—he told me he had nine sovereigns and some silver—he took the money out of his pocket, and put it out of one hand into the other—I went with him, and he bought a watch for 30 s.
Prisoner. Q. How much money do you think I had? A. You told me nine sovereigns and some odd silver—I saw gold and silver.
SOLOMON GARNETT ( police-constable M 70.) On the 29th of May I was called to a row in the court—I saw the prisoner and a number of Irishmen—the prisoner was dressed in new clothes—I never saw him so well dressed before—I took him to the station-house—this is the watch and the clothes.
Prisoner. Q. Where is the basket you took from my lodging? A. I gave it to your brother; it contained ballads and sail-needles.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been on board the Swan schooner—I then got my living by selling ballads and sail-needles—I saved between 6 l. and 7 l., with which I meant to buy some glass boxes, as a man told me I could sell them in the country—I said I would buy me a new suit of clothes, and go—I had then 3 l. 10 s., which would buy me a good stock of boxes—about an hour afterwards, there were two women having a bit of a row —one of them struck my sister—I said, "What do you hit her for? I will hit you"—if the bar-man of the public-house had been here, he would state that I was not there that day—the master came in, and found the prosecutor on the floor—there were three sailors in the house, and one asked him for a lodging; but he would not say it was me.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1584. HENRY MACKMAN and MARY NEWMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 pair of stays, value 5 s., the goods of Mary Calvert, and another; and that Mackman had been before convicted of felony.
MARY CALVERT . I live at Great Suffolk-street, Borough, and am in partenership with Eliza Morris—on the 28th of April I was standing in the shop—the prisoner Mackman came and took the stays from the door—he did not come in—he stood at the door—I ran after him a little way, but he ran so fast, I could not overtake him—he was taken in the evening—these are the stays—I did not see any body with him.
Mackman. Q. Did you see my face? A. Yes; I stood behind the counter.
ANTHONY PORKINGTON . I livc in Great Suffolk-street, next door to the prosecutor. About three o'clock, I saw the two prisoners walking together—I heard the woman say, "Now then"—the man then went, and took the stays, and ran off—I ran after him, but lost him in the Mint—I did not see Newman run off—I had seen them together about five minutes.
Mackman. Q. Where were you standing? A. At my master's shop—these stays were hanging on the door, inside—I watched you, and saw you snatch them down—then I chased you up Lant-strect—I was stopped by a lot of people.
Newman. I was not there. Witness. Yes; I am almost sure she was—I saw her at the office, and pointed her out directly.
JOHN WATERS ( police-constable M 116.) I stopped Newman in King-street, on another charge—she said, "You know me, I will come back again"—I said, "I do not like to take your word; you stopped so long the last time—I said, "You look rather bulky," and lifted up ner apron, and found these stays—she said, "I will come back again, when I have put these things up"—this was between five and six o'clock in the evening—I took Mackman on suspicion, but knew nothing of this case.
JOHN BRADLEY ( City police-constable No. 22.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Mackman's former conviction, by the name of Henry Miller, for stealing a handkerchief—I know he is the man—( read.)
MACKMAN— GUILTY .
NEWMAN— GUILTY .—Aged 17. Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350615-1585]
1585. ROBERT READ was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of November, 8 sovereigns, and 13 shillings; the monies of William Read.
WILLIAM READ . I was coachman to Captain Thomas, of Shepherd's Bush. I knew the prisoner there—he was with me on the 13th, 14th, and 15th of November—I lent him a shirt and coat—I took him to my bed-room, and laid out a clean shirt to lend him—I came down to dry a coat to put on my back, and was absent seven or eight minutes—I had opened my table-drawer that morning, to take out some money to pay for the prisoner's breakfast—I then left three sovereigns, and 13 s. in it—that was at half-past eight—he went about nine, that morning—I returned to the room, and he was gone, with the shirt—I then looked into the tabledrawer, missed the three sovereigns and 13 s.—nobody else had been in the room that morning—I lost sight of him a for long while, and met him again in Piccadilly, on the 16th of last May, and gave him in charge.
Prisoner. This was eight years ago. Witness. It was in 1827—his name is Ralph William Perry, not Read—I looked about for him, but never saw him till last May.
JOHN LOCK ( police-constable D 74.) The prisoner was given to me by the last witness, for robbing him of 3 l. 13 s.—he said he knew nothing
whatever of the money; and on taking him to the station-house, he said he went away with the shirt on his back.
(The prisoner put in a written defence, declaring his innocence, and stating that there were other persons about the premises who could have committed the robbery.)
WILLIAM READ re-examined. When I met him in Halfmoon-street, Piccadilly, he owned he had the money, and said he was going into the country on a job, and he would make it good when he came back.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18350615-1586]
1586. GEORGE BURFIELD was indicted for embezzlement.
JOSEPH ADAMS . I am a master baker. The prisoner was in my service six weeks—he took out bread—it was his duty to return an account, when he came home, of the money he might receive from the customers.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did he not state that he had lost the money? A. No; he sent his wife—she said she had but a half-crown; if I would take that, he would come to work at twelve o'clock—I said, "If he is a just man let him come and settle his accounts"—I did not refuse to take the half-crown unless I had the whole.
COURT. Q. How came his wife to come and offer you the half-crown? A. I do not know, unless it was that I should not take the law—she tendered me a letter in his own handwriting, saying he was gone off—it was about seven o'clock in the evening of the 27th of April.
LYDIA RODDA . The prosecutor served me with bread—on the 27th of April I paid the prisoner 1 s. 10 1/2 d. about eleven in the morning—I think it was some time in the forenoon—I paid it him for Mr. Adams.
SUSAN JOHNSON . On the 27th of April I paid the prisoner 5 d. I believe it was in the forenoon.
STEPHEN TAYLOR . I paid the prisoner 2 s. 3 1/2 d. in the forenoon of the 27th of April.
COURT to JOSEPH ADAMS. Q. What was the prisoner's practice about settling his accounts? A. Between twelve and one in the day. He had generally done his work by that time—he did not come home that day—I never saw him till he was taken into custody—his wife came in the evening.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a few friends, who asked me to have something to drink, and I had rather too much—I fell asleep—when I awoke I missed my money.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1587]
1587. GEORGE BURFIELD was again indicted for stealing, on the 27th of April, 10 loaves of bread, value 4 s. 2 d.; and a basket; the goods of Joseph Adams, his master.
JOSEPH ADAMS . The prisoner was in my service. He had taken seventeen loaves out on the 27th of April—seven he delivered, and ten I charge him with stealing—he had ten loaves the second time, and seven loaves the first time—he was to return at half-past twelve, to attend to his business—I never saw him till he was in custody—my wife went and found the basket at a public-house—she brought it home—I found afterwards that he was at Union-hall.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it you gave him the bread? A. No; Mrs. Adams gave it him both times—it was the same day on which the last charge is made.
MRS. ADAMS. I am the prosecutor's wife. I served the prisoner with
seven loaves, and then with ten—the first seven were to be delivered in the round in which Rodda lived, and the others in the district in which Johnson and Taylor live.
JURY. Q. Did he account for the first seven? A. No; he took seven out and then came back and had ten—the general rule is to book the bread when he has done serving all his customers—I cannot say what he had left out of the seven or out of the ten—the basket was found at the public-house.
Cross-examined. Q. You gave the loaves to the prisoner? A. Yes; I did not give him directions to distribute them in the different walks because he knows them—I will not undertake to swear that he might not deliver these loaves, or some of them, to Rodda, Johnson, and Taylor.
COURT to JOSEPH ADAMS. Q. What was the 1 s. 10 1/2 d.? A. That was for what she had had the latter end of the week, and what they had that day.
LYDIA RODDA re-examined. I only had one 4lb. and one 21b. loaf that day.
SUSAN JOHNSON . I had two leaves.
COURT to STEPHEN TAYLOR. I cannot say how many I had—I am certain I had not less than one loaf.
Prisoner. I got intoxicated and left the basket at the public-house while I went to get the money. While I was gone he sent two officers to my house—I thought I would rather not be taken, and went further away—I was afterwards returning to get the money to send him, but they had me taken.
NOT GUILTY .
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1599. JOHN CRUTCHER was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of June, 1 hat, value 2 s.; and 1 box, value 2 d.; the goods of James Morris.
JAMES MORRIS . I am a bricklayer's labourer, and live at Putney in Surrey. On the 11th of June, I went into the fair-field at Wandsworth, where I had some swings at work—I gave the prisoner, who was with me, my jacket and waistcoat to hold—I told him there was a tobacco-box in the pocket which had a duplicate of a hat in it—he gave me the jacket back again without the box and duplicate—I went to the pawnbroker's the next morning, and my hat had been fetched out.
Prisoner. The clothes were throwing about the field—I picked up this tobacco-box in Mr. Robert's booth.
Witness. I am sure the box was in the jacket pocket when I gave it to him.

1600. JOHN ALEXANDER JOHNSON and ALFRED BARTLETT were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 2 spoons, value 13 s., the goods of Thomas Martin Ready, the master of the said John Alexander Johnson.
REV. THOMAS MARTIN READY . I live at Kennington. The prisoner Johnson was in my employ up to the 25th of March—he was employed to clean knives and shoes—some spoons of mine were missing, and on inquiry being made they were found at the pawnbroker's—I know the spoons to be mine—I know nothing of Bartlett.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What are your Christian names? A. Thomas Martin—I am a clergyman of the Church of England.
JOHN READY . I am the prosecutor's brother. I went to the pawnbroker, and found the spoons—I had no communication with either of the prisoners.
EDWARD LEWIS . I am in the employ of Mr. Matthews', a pawnbroker, at Kennington. I produce four spoons, one table-spoon, and one tea-spoon which I took in of a person, whom I believe to be Johnson, on the 24th of March—a dessert spoon and a tea-spoon were pawned on the 20th of March, but I did not take them in—I know Bartlett; he came on the 18th of May, about the spoons, which had been pawned on the 20th of March, and on the 24th of March, he had another 1 l. on them all, and they were all put into one duplicate then.
EDWARD BIRCH . I live at Mr. Matthews'. I took in the dessert-spoon and the tea-spoon, on the 20th of March, I believe, of Johnson.
WILLIAM ALDERMAN ( police-constable Z 168.) I took the two prisoners on the 26th of May. I found the duplicate of the whole of this plate on Johnson—he said, he found it in Mr. Ready's dust-hole, and pawned it at different times—Bartlett said, he knew nothing of Johnson, or of the plate.
JOHNSON— GUILTY . Aged 18.
BARTLETT— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350615-1601]
1601. JOHN ALEXANDER JOHNSON and ALFRED BARTLETT were again indicted, for stealing, on the 24th of March, 2 spoons, value 20 s., the goods of Thomas Martin Ready, the master of John Alexander Johnson.
REV. THOMAS MARTIN READY . Johnson was in my service—some of my family missed the spoons—I am not certain when—these two spoons are mine.
EDWARDLEWIS. I live with Mr. Matthews, a pawnbroker. I took in these spoons, on the 24th of March, I think of Johnson.
Johnson's Defence ( written.) I am entirely innocent of stealing the—they had been wrapped up in a piece of paper, and put into the dust-hole—I had to go to the dust-hole to sift cinders every morning, and in turning over the dust, I found the spoons, and I intended to have got them out, and return them to Mr. Ready—Alfred Bartlett had the ticket on Monday morning, and went and got more money on them, quite without my knowledge, on the 18th of May.
JOHNSON— GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
BARTLETT— NOT GUILTY .
Third Jury, before Mr. Serjeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350615-1602]
1602. WILLIAM MENDS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of June, 13 handkerchiefs, value 2 l. 15 s., and 1 pair of gloves, value 2 s.; the goods of Frederick Hopkins, his master.
FREDERICK HOPKINS . I am a linen-draper, and live at No. 219, Borough. The prisoner had been my porter for above 18 months—having occasion to suspect him, I desired him, on the 11th of June, to bring down his box—he made no reply, but brought down his box from where it was kept, on the third floor, to my sitting-room, which is on the first door—he opened it, and I found in it six pairs of stockings, which I considered to be my property, and one silk handkerchief with a character on it which I use in my business, and which was put on by one of the witnesses—a bag was afterwards found on the landing even with my sitting-room, which contained twelve silk handkerchiefs and some other articles, which I believe to be mine; but five hours had elapsed between my finding this property in the bag, and the prisoner had been in custody. I had not seen the bag there before, and there was no dust upon it—it did not appear to have laid there long.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. I believe the prisoner was quitting your service? A. Yes—he made some demur at my stopping part of his wages for breaking some glass, which I considered was done carelessly—I found his hat near this bag, with this pair of gloves in it—this gownpiece and twelve handkerchiefs were found in the bag—they have my mark on them—I know they are mine—they had been in my shop the night previous—this black silk handkerchief I do not swear to myself.
FREDERICK POWELL . I live with Mr. Hopkins. I know this blue bag—it belonged to the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it? A. By seeing him use it, and no one else—it is linen, and has a red cord to it, which is rather peculiar—I had not seen the prisoner use it that morning.
JOSEPH NEW . I am shopman to the prosecutor. I know this black silk handkerchief—it has my mark on it—I generally buy these things myself.
Cross-examined. Q. It appears to have been worn? A. Yes; but the mark is not worn out—every black silk handkerchief that is sold has not this mark on it—I generally mark the outside one of the piece—I cannot swear that this has not been sold, but the prisoner had not bought it.
Prisoner's Defence. The things that were in my box were mine before I was in his employ—I expected persons here to prove it.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1603. JAMES BUUROWS was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of June, 6 shillings and 6 sixpences, the monies of James Kirby, from his person, and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES KIRBY . I am ostler at the Ram Inn, Wandsworth. On Wednesday night, the 10th of June, I was in the tap-room, between twelve and one o'clock—I was tired, and fell asleep—the prisoner was in the tap-room, on my right hand side—I had 10 s. 6 d. in my right-hand pocket—a person asked me to lend him a shilling—I got out some silver, and threw him a shilling across the table—I then put my money into my pocket again, and laid on the table—I slept about half an hour—I then awoke with some disturbance—the prisoner passed by my side, and said, "Being fair time, you may as well have something to drink, and we will have a song"—he got a pot of beer—I drank, and was then going up to bed—I then missed my money—I came down the next morning, and enquired about it, and who should I see, but Jack Morn, to whom I had lent the shilling the night before—there had been no one on the side of the table where I was when I lost my money.
JAMES WILLIS . I was in the tap-room on that Wednesday evening—I saw the prosecutor there, and the prisoner's hand was in his pocket.
ALFRED WITTS ( police-constable V 32.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction—( read)—I know he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

[trialAccount t18350615-1605]
1605. DANIEL GOTHARD was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 1 trunk, value 5 s.; 1 portmanteau, value 5 s.; 7 shirts, value 20 s.; 1 coat, value 3 l.; 4 caps, value 2 s.; 6 pair of stockings, &c.; the goods of John Pontey.
JOHN PONTEY . I am a seedsman. I was in the King's Bench prison—I knew the prisoner in the country, about five years ago—he came to visit me in the King's Bench, to ask how I did—I do not know any other reason—I left the prison on the Thursday previous to Good Friday, which was on the 17th of April—the prisoner accompanied me that day—I took my trunk and my portmanteau—I afterwards lost them—they contained the articles stated in the indictment—I removed them to the New Bridge Hotel, kept by Mr. Wallis, at the foot of London Bridge—I left them there on the Saturday morning—I stopped there from the Thursday night till the Saturday morning—the prisoner parted with me on the Thursday evening; but he called again on me on the Friday evening—not a word passed about the trunks, at any time—he was not to interfere or take any notice of them—I got into Yorkshire by the steam packet—I had been out early, and was coming over the bridge—the vessel was going off—I ran into the hotel—I said, "I ill leave my trunks and the bill, unpaid—give me one of your bar bills and I will write upon
it that you may know it again—I went to Huddersfield—I got there two days afterwards—I wrote up immediately, and sent by the guard—I did not receive my trunks, and I came to London—I then heard the trunks had been removed—I went to the station-house on the Monday week—I there saw the prisoner—the policeman went and got him—I afterwards found the trunk and the portmanteau at the prisoner's house—they had been opened—they were locked when I left them—both the locks had been broken.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you serious in saying you do not know why this man called on you in the King's Bench? A. Yes, quite serious; after he had called on me, I employed him to seck out an attorney—I do not remember the name of the attorney—I mentioned the name to the policeman, but now I have forgotten it. I was not acquainted with him enough to know his name—I found his name by information that I had given me—it is an odd name—Mackarel is the name—I have seen him—the prisoner found him out for me, and he did what I wanted done—I paid the expenses myself—the prisoner only went to Hoxton for him—I had plenty of money when I left the prison—I had had the property in the trunks several weeks—it was put in at different times—it would be impossible to say when I bought the property—I took the benefit of the Insolvent Act the day before Good Friday—this was not the property of the assignees—nothing was in my other trunk but old invoices—it was a portmanteau—there were two or three hundred invoices—I returned this property in my schedule—it is not the property of my assignees—I did not swear that this was not my property—the question was not put to me—I never swore it was not my property—I was going to Huddersfield—I called at a place named Goole—I did not tell the prisoner that I should have only 7 s. 6 d. left, when I left Goole, and desire him to pay my expenses at the tavern—I swore to my shedule, and these trunks were in the room when the person came to take an inventory of what I had—he saw them I read the schedule before I swore to it—whatever is in the schedule is correct—it is a good while ago—I cannot tell whether the trunks were in it or not—they were, to the best of my knowledge—I would not swear it—I swear I never gave the prisoner authority to go and fetch these things—there were no money transactions between us—he had no business to pay Mr. Wallis—he did not know what was in the trunk or portmanteau—he got them both—the invoices were of no value to anybody—I had known the prisoner five years—I do not know how he knew I was in the Bench—he saw my name, very likely, in the newspaper—it was two or three days before I came out, that I employed him to go for Mr. Mackarel—I never received any letter from the prisoner down in Yorkshire—Mr. Wallis told me that the prisoner had paid him 7 s. 8 d.—in two separate sums—he paid 4 s., and got the trunk, and then he got the portmanteau, and paid the remainder—I know Squires and Tolkington, but I paid them all that was due—I have the receipt in my pocket—I heard the prisoner say that he had written to me, but I never received his letter, certainly.
JOHN PRIOR . I live at Mr. Wallis', at the foot of London-bridge. On we 16th of April, the prosecutor and the prisoner came there in the cab—they brought a trunk and portmanteau—I saw the prisoner there on the Friday—the prosecutor left on Saturday morning early—he asked my mistress, in my presence, for a bill of the place—the trunk and portmanteau were left there—he wrote his name on the back of one of our bar-bills—he
left in a hurry—the prisoner came the same day about one o'clock—he asked for his friend—I said he was not in—he made a remark that he was in the King's Bench—I said "I thought there was something, as he got up early this morning and yesterday morning:" he replied," O yes, they have been looking after him, it is term time"—he had a pint of ale, and desired it to be put down to the prosecutor's bill; saying, he would see him in the course of the afternoon, and he durst say he, or some one else, would come and pay the bill, and take the goods away—he came again, and paid part of the bill; which was 7 s. 8 d., and took away the trunk—he then came again and paid the remainder, and took the portmanteau—there was a friend waiting for him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you lift the portmanteau? A. Not in giving it to him—I brought them both down stairs, but I did not take notice of their weight—I had not known the prisoner nor Mr. Pontey before he came to lodge at the house—he went off in such a hurry, that I suspected there was something amiss.
JOHN PONTEY re-examined. Q. Had you these goods in the King's Bench? A. Yes; I brought them from the King's Bench—I had been there seven weeks.
FRANCIS FAGAN . I am a police-inspector. I was applied to by the prosecutor—I went to the B division, New-way, Westminster, and sent for the prisoner (who was a police officer) off his beat, and took him into custody—I asked him what he had done with Mr. Pontey's things—he said he had sent them to Yorkshire—I asked him if he bad booked them—he said he did not recollect—he could not tell me by what coach he sent them, or by what waggon—he afterwards said he sent them to Islington—I went to the street where he said he lodged, near Finsbury-square; and found in his lodgings thirty-seven duplicates; but only four relating to the property here—I spoke to him on the subject, and he said his children were starving, and that induced him to commit what he did—I made inquiry, and believe that to be the fact—his wife was in very great distress at that time—I have reason to believe they were starving—he came into the police on the 1st of June—this was committed on the 18th of April.
WILLIAM CHAPMAN . I am a pawnbroker. I have a shirt pawned for 1 s. 6 d., on the 23d of April, in the name of Ann Gothard—this is the duplicate I gave.
ADAM REED . I am a pawnbroker. I have a coat, a watch, two shirts, 4 handkerchiefs, and 2 razors; these are the duplicates, but I did not take them in.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I have known the prosecutor many years—there is not one article that is in his schedule—the trunk is not in his schedule—I was to keep it back—he sent for me two or three days running—I went to search for the attorney—I spent nearly 2 l. in seeking him, and paying him to come—on Good Friday night he told me he had only 7 s. 6 d., and I was to pay his bill and take up the trunks—I wrote to the prosecutor, wishing him to send word how I was to send them—he came to London, and gave me in charge—he knew very well that he had received a letter; and he knew that I was applying to get on the police—I did get on; and he came and had me taken—I told him that I did not deny that I had got the things; and if he would go with me to my old master's he should have them returned.
(James Hucking, of the Britannia Brewery, Little Harcourt-street, and
Mrs. Meredith, 19, Earl-street, a widow, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 31 .—Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18350615-1606]
1606. CAROLINE SEYMOUR was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of June, 1 Petticoat, vale 1 s., the goods of Emma White; 1 napkin, value 6 d.; the goods of Sarah Rowbottom; 2 gowns, value 3 s.; 1 apron, value 4.; and 1 pinafore, value 6 d., the goods of William Wyatt, her master.
SARAH WYATT . The prisoner was in my service for about a fortnight—I missed these articles ( looking at them)—I had seen them on the second day of her coming into the house, which was on the 12th of May—I missed them a few days afterwards.
Prisoner. Q. Can you show me the two dresses you have brought against me? A. These are them—I had them made for myself—they were dresses; but they are torn apart—I left them safe.
Prisoner. I wrote to master, begging him to take it into his consideration to come forward, and say that part of it was his sister's, Sarah Rowbottoms—that napkin in is one belonging to my children.
COURT to S. WYATT. Q. Have you any mark on that piece of gown? A. Yes—the making of it, and the apron too—I know it by this band—I am sure it is my husband's, William Wyatt's, property—they are not worth more than 6 d. altogether.
Prisoner. Did you not, (on your oath) when I washed up all my aprons, say, "Ann, tie that apron before you, that you don't take cold?"—you know you are insane; you are not fit to take an oath—while I was in custody, you went to my lodings, and took things of mine out of my box to bring against me—you even took balls of tape and worsted.
BENJAMIN SKELTON . I am a pawnbroker. I Produce this gown and a napkin, pawned by the prisoner—the gown, I believe, is her own property.
EMMA WHITE . This petticoat is mine—it is marked—it was in my box, which was locked.
Prisoner. I did pawn them, but White was the bar-maid, and she asked me to go and bring a wreath of flowers for her, which I did, and some white-stain ribbon, which is on her bonnet now—she gave me 2 s. for the wreath, and they cost 2 s. 6 d.—she said, "Never mind, I will pay you"—but she never did. Witness. Yes, I did.
Prisoner. When I went to bed at night, you said, "There is a white petticoat you may have." Witness. No; I never did.
Prisoner. She did so, on my oath, if I might take my oath; and this apron Mrs. Wyatt lent me—White gave me the petticoat, with a pair of stockings.
(The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the prosecutrix had given her the apron.)
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350615-1607]
1607. WILLIAM WALLER was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of June, 1 shilling, the monies of Edward Grove, his master.
EDWARD GROVE . I keep a shop at Lambeth. The Prisoner was in my employ as an errand-boy—I marked some money, and put it into my
till—I afterwards examined the prisoner, and found this shilling, which I had marked, and which was missing from the till.
DANIEL HAGGERTY ( police-constable L 26.) On the 19th of June, the prosecutor told me he had lost a marked shilling from his till—we searched the prisoner, and found this marked shilling in his fob.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 12.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor. Confined Nine Days, and Whipped.—
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JULY 6.
